❯ <pasted_content id="d4c8">
  According to the proposal presentation, the mentor commented on my component.
  It is: "Clearly define the exact threats to be detected, such as fire,
  smoke, unauthorised access, or suspicious activities. The system should also
  determine the threat severity, whether vehicles or people are affected, and
  which responsible parties should be notified for each type of incident."

  So, based on this comment, how should I update my component?
  Please give me all updates step by step.
  And tell me how I can manage this easily, and if I want to change the model
  training part according to the comment, please tell me suitable guidance step
  by step.
  According to this panel's comment, if I want to retrain this model again, how
  many hours should I spend on that training part with Colab free GPU service?
  If I buy Colab, how much time should I spend? If I buy Colab, I am planning
  to buy 9.99$ plan. I think it is enough to train my model according to this
  comment's update.
  So give me your solution step by step, very clearly and easy to understand.
  </pasted_content id="d4c8">

⏺ Short answer

  The mentor's comment is mainly about what happens after detection: which
  threats exist, how serious each is, who or what is affected, and who gets
  told. That belongs in your rule engine and alert design (stages 2–3), not in
  the YOLO model. Your current model already detects everything needed: person,
  vehicle, weapon, fire/smoke, tampering, loitering and face_covered.

  So retraining isn't required to answer the comment. There's one optional model
  change worth considering: splitting fire_smoke into fire and smoke. The
  mentor names them separately, and they deserve different severities.

  ---

  Part 1: How to update the component (step by step)

  Step 1: Define the exact threats (a threat catalogue)

  ┌─────┬──────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  #  │      Threat      │               How it's detected                │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ T1  │ Fire             │ model: fire (or fire_smoke) box                │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ T2  │ Smoke (no        │ model: smoke box (needs the split; see Part 2) │
  │     │ visible flame)   │                                                │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ T3  │ Weapon presence  │ model: weapon box                              │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │     │ Vehicle          │ model: tampering box, or rule: a person        │
  │ T4  │ tampering /      │ touching a vehicle for longer than X seconds   │
  │     │ theft            │                                                │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │     │ Unauthorised     │ rule: a person inside a restricted zone (bay,  │
  │ T5  │ access           │ parts store) outside allowed times, or without │
  │     │                  │  a staff assignment                            │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │     │ Loitering near a │ model: loitering box, or rule: a person near   │
  │ T6  │  vehicle         │ the same vehicle for longer than Y seconds     │
  │     │                  │ (DeepSORT time)                                │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ T7  │ Concealed        │ model: face_covered, used mainly to raise the  │
  │     │ identity         │ severity of T4–T6                              │
  └─────┴──────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Step 2: Define the severity levels

  Use 4 levels: Low → Medium → High → Critical. Give each threat a base level,
  then add modifiers:

  ┌─────────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Threat      │   Base     │           Raised one level if…           │
  │                 │  severity  │                                          │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fire            │ Critical   │ (already the highest)                    │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Smoke           │ High       │ it's on or next to a vehicle, or lasts   │
  │                 │            │ longer than 10 s → Critical              │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Weapon          │ Critical   │ (already the highest)                    │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tampering       │ High       │ face covered, at night/after hours, or   │
  │                 │            │ 2+ people → Critical                     │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Unauthorised    │ Medium     │ after hours, face covered, or entering a │
  │ access          │            │  bay with a customer vehicle → High      │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loitering       │ Low        │ face covered, near a customer vehicle,   │
  │                 │            │ or repeated → Medium/High                │
  └─────────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Add two false-alarm guards:
  - Persistence: the threat must be seen in N consecutive frames (e.g. 2
    seconds) before an alert fires.
  - Confidence: a minimum detection confidence for each class.

  Step 3: Decide what's affected (vehicles or people)

  Use box positions plus Component 1's bay.state:
  - Vehicle affected: the threat box overlaps or is close to a vehicle box. Use
    bay.state to identify which customer vehicle, bay and job.
  - People affected: person boxes near the threat (e.g. people close to a fire,
    or near someone carrying a weapon). Count them.
  - Both / none: e.g. smoke in an empty corridor = none affected, so the
    severity stays lower.

  Step 4: Decide who gets notified (a notification matrix)

  ┌──────────┬────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Severity │    Who is notified     │                 How                 │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Low      │ logged only            │ dashboard                           │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Medium   │ security guard         │ dashboard alert                     │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │          │ guard + bay supervisor │                                     │
  │ High     │  + centre manager;     │ dashboard + message                 │
  │          │ vehicle owner after    │                                     │
  │          │ staff confirm          │                                     │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │          │ everyone above +       │ a staff member makes the call after │
  │ Critical │ emergency services     │  confirming (human-in-the-loop, to  │
  │          │ (fire brigade /        │ avoid false emergency calls)        │
  │          │ police)                │                                     │
  └──────────┴────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  Map each threat type to its parties as well:
  - Fire/smoke: guard, manager, fire service, vehicle owner.
  - Weapon: guard, manager, police.
  - Tampering: guard, bay supervisor, vehicle owner.
  - Unauthorised access and loitering: guard.

  Step 5: Extend the security.alert event

  Add these fields so other components and the dashboard can act:
  - threat_type, severity, confidence;
  - affected_vehicles (bay, job/plate from bay.state) and affected_persons
    (count);
  - notify_roles, camera_id, zone, timestamp, snapshot;
  - status (open / acknowledged / resolved).

  Step 6: Put the rules in a config file

  Keep the threats, severities, modifiers, zones and notification matrix in one
  file (e.g. configs/threat_rules.yaml). Changing a rule then needs no code
  changes and no retraining. This is the easy way to manage it.

  Step 7: Update the documents and evaluation

  - Proposal/report: add the threat catalogue, severity table and notification
    matrix (Steps 1–4).
  - Evaluation:
    - model: mAP per class;
    - rule engine: correct severity and correct "affected" decision on a set of
      test clips;
    - end to end: alert delay (seconds) and false alarms per hour.

  ---

  Part 2: Do you need to change the model? (optional)

  My recommendation: keep the current model for the rule-engine work. Do one
  retraining only if you want fire and smoke as separate threats, because a
  smoke-only alert should be High while fire is Critical.

  This split is feasible without new labelling. Most of your sources already
  label fire and smoke separately (6k, Fire-Smoke-CCTV, cctv security); Car
  Fires and fire-dataset label fire only.

  Steps, if you choose it:
  1. Class map: in configs/class_map.yaml, map fire → fire and smoke → smoke.
     That makes 8 classes: person, vehicle, weapon, fire, smoke, tampering,
     loitering, face_covered.
  2. Rebuild: run python scripts/run_pipeline.py. The build is deterministic, so
     the images and splits stay the same.
  3. Re-apply the auto-added boxes: take them from auto_boxes.csv in your
     dataset_autolabelled.zip, so you don't need to auto-label again. I can
     write this step.
  4. Upload the new zip to Drive under a new name.
  5. Colab: set a new RUN_NAME (e.g. c4_yolo11s_v2), and start from your current
     best.pt instead of yolo11s.pt. It already knows people, vehicles and
     weapons, so ~30 epochs is usually enough (an estimate).
  6. Compare the new test results with the current model before switching.

  You don't need new classes for unauthorised access, severity or "affected":
  those are rules.

  ---

  Part 3: Training time and cost

  For exact numbers: open MyDrive/component4/runs/c4_yolo11s/results.csv. The
  time column shows the minutes per epoch of your run. The figures below are
  estimates based on about 6–10 minutes per epoch on a T4.

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │           │                  │    30-epoch fine-tune     │  60-epoch    │
  │   Plan    │       GPU        │       (recommended)       │    full      │
  │           │                  │                           │   training   │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Free      │ T4, sessions     │ ~3–5 h of GPU, spread     │ ~6–10 h,     │
  │ Colab     │ ~1.5–2 h, daily  │ over ~2–4 days (resume    │ over ~4–7    │
  │           │ limit            │ each time)                │ days         │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Paid      │ T4 or L4, longer │ T4: ~3–5 h ≈ 6–10 units;  │ T4: ~6–10 h  │
  │ Colab     │  sessions while  │ L4 (~2× faster): ~1.5–2.5 │ ≈ 12–20      │
  │ ($9.99)   │ units last       │  h ≈ 8–12 units           │ units        │
  └───────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Is $9.99 enough?

  Yes, comfortably. The $9.99 option gives about 100 compute units. A T4 uses
  roughly 1.5–2 units per hour (approximate; Colab shows the exact rate in the
  Resources panel). That's enough for the fine-tune plus a full retrain plus
  spare. Pricing changed with "Google AI Plans", so check what the current $9.99
  plan includes before buying.

  Before you buy

  1. It conflicts with your project rule. CLAUDE.md says the project is zero
     cost, so paying for Colab is a change to that rule. It's your decision, and
     I'd log it if you go ahead.
  2. Free alternative: Kaggle Notebooks. Usually ~30 free GPU hours per week
     with longer sessions. That fits a 30-epoch fine-tune in one or two
     sessions.
  3. Keep the tab open. On the paid plan the session can still stop if the tab
     closes; background running is only in higher plans.

  ---

  My suggested order

  1. Now (no training): write the threat catalogue, severity rules and
     notification matrix. That answers the mentor directly.
  2. Build the rule engine on your current model (tracking, zones, severity,
     "affected", notifications).
  3. Optional: the fire/smoke split as a 30-epoch fine-tune.
  4. Then: Phase 5 (review the auto-added boxes), and the final training run.

  If you'd like, I can draft the threat catalogue document and
  configs/threat_rules.yaml next, or prepare the fire/smoke split rebuild.

❯ My problem is how to identify, while loitering, whether a person is a worker,
  a suspicious person, or a customer. I want your answer and the best
  solution: a cost-free, easy-to-deploy solution.
  I expect to pay only for Colab.

⏺ Short answer

  Don't try to label a person as "suspicious" from how they look. A camera can't
  reliably tell that, and judging people by appearance is unfair and causes
  false alarms. Split it into two simpler questions:

  1. Who are they (their role)? Worker, customer, or unknown visitor.
  2. What are they doing (their behaviour)? Loitering, tampering, entering
     restricted areas, face covered, at night.

  A person becomes "suspicious" only when their behaviour doesn't match their
  role. For example, a worker standing next to a car in a bay for 20 minutes is
  normal, but an unknown person doing the same is suspicious.

  Step 1: Recognise workers by their uniform (the best free option)

  Most service centres already give staff a uniform or a high-visibility vest.
  Make it a rule that staff on the floor wear it, then teach the model one extra
  class: worker (a person wearing the staff uniform or vest).

  How to get training data for free:
  - Own photos: 300–500 photos of your own staff in uniform at the service
    centre. The model needs to learn your uniform, so this matters most. Keep
    these photos private.
  - Public data: free hi-vis vest / PPE datasets on Roboflow Universe help the
    model learn vests in general.
  - Labelling: box each uniformed person as worker. They also keep their person
    box.

  Cost: one extra training run on Colab (a fine-tune, about 30 epochs). This is
  the only paid part.

  Why this is best: no faces, no ID cards to scan, no extra hardware, and it
  works on your existing cameras.

  Step 2: Recognise customers with information you already have, not the camera

  Customers don't wear anything special, so use context instead:
  - Where they are: people in the reception or waiting area are treated as
    customers.
  - Their vehicle: Component 1's bay.state already knows which vehicle belongs
    to which job. If a person walks straight from reception to their own vehicle
    with a staff member nearby, that's normal.
  - Simple rule for the system: any person who isn't a worker is a "visitor".
    Visitors are allowed in public areas (reception, waiting area, car park) but
    not in work bays or parts stores without a worker nearby.

  Step 3: Decide what's suspicious using behaviour rules

  DeepSORT already gives each person an ID and tracks how long they stay.
  Combine that with the role:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │                     Situation                     │       Result        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Worker in a bay, any duration                     │ Normal, no alert    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Visitor in the waiting area                       │ Normal              │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Visitor near a vehicle for more than ~2 minutes   │ Loitering alert     │
  │ with no worker nearby                             │ (Medium)            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Visitor inside a work bay or restricted zone with │ Unauthorised access │
  │  no worker within a few metres                    │  (High)             │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Visitor touching a vehicle door/window, or a      │ Tampering (High)    │
  │ tampering detection                               │                     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Any of the above plus face covered, after hours,  │ Raise one level     │
  │ or 2+ visitors together                           │                     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Weapon detected, whoever it is                    │ Critical            │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  You can tune the times and distances in a config file without retraining.

  Step 4: Handle mistakes safely

  - Worker without a vest: treated as a visitor, so they may cause an alert. The
    guard dismisses it, and you enforce the uniform rule.
  - Someone wearing a stolen vest: a known weakness. Mention it in your report
    as a limitation, and note that face recognition was deliberately not used
    for privacy reasons.
  - Every alert goes to a human first (guard or supervisor) before anyone is
    called. The system flags; a person decides.

  What I don't recommend

  - Face recognition of staff and customers. It needs consent and storage of
    biometric data, raises privacy and legal issues, and is harder to build.
  - A "suspicious person" class trained on how people look. It's unreliable and
    biased.
  - Paid ID systems (RFID badges, special cameras). They add cost and hardware.

  Summary of changes to your component

  1. Model: add one class, worker (uniform/vest). Retrain once on Colab with
     your own uniform photos.
  2. Zones: draw areas on each camera view (reception, waiting area, bays, parts
     store) in a config file.
  3. Rule engine:
     - role = worker or visitor (person box + worker box);
     - behaviour = time near a vehicle, zone, touching, face covered, time of
       day;
     - output = threat type and severity.
  4. Use bay.state from Component 1 to know which vehicle belongs to which job.
  5. Report: explain that "suspicious" is decided from behaviour plus role, not
     appearance, and list the limitations.

  When you're ready, I can prepare the worker class addition, covering the
  labelling steps and the class-map change, and draft the zone and rule
  configuration file.

  you want to change or add images, please provide resource links; I will

  thing is to identify vehicle service centre premises where people are
  open-face covering persons or try to open doors or windows using tools. I

⏺ Part 1: Changes needed in the current datasets (excluding missing boxes)

  These are labelling meaning problems, where a class means different things in
  different sources. Auto-labelling in Colab won't fix them, so fix them before
  the final training.

  #: 1
  Problem: tampering includes house break-ins, not just vehicles. Thief
  detection
  (archive (1)) shows people forcing house doors (doorbell cameras), labelled
  human_brakingdoor / human_lockunlocking → tampering.
  Where: thief-detection, 2,364 images
  What to do: Decide: keep as general "break-in" (more data, but the model may
  learn that any door is a target), or map those two classes to drop and keep
  only person/weapon/face_covered. My recommendation: drop them as tampering,
  because your threat is tampering with a vehicle.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Problem: tampering boxes have two styles. stealing (vehicle theft detection)
  boxes cover car + person; theft and breaking_in box only the person.
  Where: vehicle theft detection, 492 images
  What to do: Re-box stealing around the person only (about 490 boxes) in
  Colab/CVAT, or drop it as tampering. Mixed styles confuse the model.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Problem: face_covered has two styles. hiding_face1, Theft mask and face hiding

  box the head; hiding_face2 boxes the whole body.
  Where: Surveillance, 585 images
  What to do: Re-box hiding_face2 around the head (617 boxes), or map it to
  drop.
  The head is the right box for "face covered".
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Problem: Spying merged into loitering. Spying in Surveillance shows peeping
  and
  crouching, which is closer to tampering behaviour.
  Where: Surveillance, 146 boxes
  What to do: Fine to keep in loitering. Only change it if validation shows
  confusion.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Problem: Loitering comes from one place and a few actors. 576 images, all from

  Surveillance phone videos. Loitering Detection gives only person boxes.
  Where: Surveillance
  What to do: Add your own staged loitering clips (Part 3). This is the weakest
  class.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Problem: Tools aren't labelled anywhere. Crowbars, slim jims and screwdrivers
  at car doors appear in theft (e.g. "robber-with-a-crowbar") but aren't boxed.
   Knives and swords are weapon, but a crowbar isn't.
  Where: theft, vehicle theft, thief-detection
  What to do: Decide: add a break_in_tool class, or leave tools out and rely on
  tampering (a person at a door/window). Recommendation: leave tools out of the
   model. Tools are small, varied and rare in your data, and the tampering rule
   already covers "a person forcing a door".
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Problem: No staff vs visitor data. No worker (uniform/vest) images.
  Where: none
  What to do: New data is needed (Part 3).
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  Where: Knife Detection, Suspicious Activity
  What to do: Keep them for now. Cap weapon so it doesn't dominate (it's 12,455
  vs loitering 576).

  Part 3: Data to add, with links

  Search the pages below and download datasets in YOLOv11 format, preferring
  ones without augmentation. These are search pages; I can't guarantee any
  specific dataset there stays available or has a usable licence, so check each
  one's licence before downloading.

  1. worker (uniform / high-visibility vest)
  - Roboflow Universe, "safety vest":
    https://universe.roboflow.com/search?q=safety%20vest
  - Roboflow Universe, "construction site safety" (vest + person):
    https://universe.roboflow.com/search?q=construction%20site%20safety
  - Kaggle, "safety vest detection":
    https://www.kaggle.com/search?q=safety+vest+detection
  - Most important: 300–500 photos of your own staff in uniform, taken in the
    service centre. Keep them private (own_staged__staff).

  2. More loitering / tampering / face covered (CCTV style)
  - https://universe.roboflow.com/search?q=car%20theft
  - https://universe.roboflow.com/search?q=balaclava
  - https://universe.roboflow.com/search?q=loitering
  - https://www.kaggle.com/search?q=car+theft+cctv

  3. Own staged clips (the best data, at no cost): record short videos with
  friends at the service centre or a car park:
  - someone walking around a car, peering into windows (loitering, 2–3 minutes
    per clip);
  - trying door handles, using a fake tool (tampering);
  - wearing a mask or hood (face_covered);
  - staff in vests doing normal work (worker).

  Extract 1 frame per second and label them. Even 500–1,000 frames from 20–30
  clips helps a lot.

  When you download, put each dataset in data/raw/ with a name like
  worker__rf_safety-vest, then tell me. I'll audit it and add it with
  03b_add_sources.py the same way as before.

  Highest-accuracy settings (already in the notebook)

  - Model: yolo11m, a larger and more accurate model than last time's yolo11s.
  - Training: up to 100 epochs, early stopping after 25 epochs with no
    improvement, and automatic batch size.

     notebook → scripts/colab/Component4_YOLO11_Training.ipynb.
  4. Buy the plan, then choose Runtime → Change runtime type → T4 GPU (or L4,
     see below) → Save.
  5. Runtime → Run all, and allow the Drive connection.
     - Step 2 should print CUDA available: True.
     - Step 5 should print Auto-labelling already done on the Mac - skipped.
     - Step 8 starts training.
  6. After the first epoch, run Step 8b with the units-per-hour value from the
     Resources panel. It prints your real remaining hours and units.
  7. If the session stops: Runtime → Change runtime type → GPU, then Run all.
     Training resumes from the last epoch.
  8. When training is done: Step 9 gives per-class test results, and Step 10
     exports best.pt and an ONNX copy to
     MyDrive/component4_v2/runs/c4_v2_yolo11m/weights/.

  Add about 1 unit per session for setup (copying and unzipping the dataset).

  My recommendation: use the T4. It uses the fewest units and leaves about half
  of the ~100 units ($9.99) spare for a second run after reviewing the labels.
  Pick the L4 only if you want it finished in fewer sessions. These numbers are
  estimates; Step 8b shows the real ones after epoch 1.

    2, and review later for the final model.

    tracking time is the main loitering signal; the threat catalogue says so.

❯ I have a, question can I fully train this model completely using this Mac
  book if can do it how much time want to do it and easily can setup
  environment to that model training part.
  I only want your answer; that's enough.

⏺ Yes, it can be done, but it's slow. The setup is easy, because your .venv
  already has everything installed (Ultralytics, PyTorch with Mac GPU support).
  It needs no extra installs and no cost. Training is one command from the
  project folder:

  .venv/bin/yolo detect train data=data/merged/data.yaml model=yolo11m.pt
  epochs=100 patience=25 imgsz=640 batch=8 device=mps workers=4
  project=models/runs name=c4_v2_mac

  If it stops, resume with .venv/bin/yolo detect train resume
  model=models/runs/c4_v2_mac/weights/last.pt.

  Estimated time on your MacBook. This is a rough estimate. I measured your
  Mac's GPU at about 0.18 s per image for YOLO11x detection; training costs
  roughly 3× that per image, scaled down for the smaller models:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │           Model            │   Per    │ ~70 epochs (early  │   100    │
  │                            │  epoch   │       stop)        │  epochs  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ yolo11m (the most accurate │ ~1.5–2 h │ ~5–6 days non-stop │ ~7–8     │
  │  setup)                    │          │                    │ days     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ yolo11s (lighter)          │ ~30–40   │ ~1.5–2 days        │ ~2–3     │
  │                            │ min      │                    │ days     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Colab T4, for comparison   │ ~14 min  │ ~16 h              │ ~23 h    │
  │ (yolo11m)                  │          │                    │          │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────────────┴──────────┘

  Downsides of training on the Mac:
  - Memory is the limit. Your Mac GPU allows about 9 GB, which ran out earlier
    during prediction. So the batch size must stay small (8), which is slower
    and can be slightly less accurate.
  - The Mac must stay awake and plugged in for days. It will run hot, and other
    work will be slow. Use caffeinate -i to stop it sleeping.
  - Training on the Mac GPU is less mature than on an NVIDIA GPU. Mixed
    precision is off, and runs are occasionally unstable. Results are usually
    similar, but a few points lower is possible.

  My opinion: the Mac is fine for yolo11s (about 2 days), or for a short test
  run of a few epochs. For the highest-accuracy yolo11m model, Colab is the
  better choice: about 1 day of GPU time for roughly 30–40 units.

         + |                                                             
      59 +| L4 (~4.8 units/h) | ~7 | ~8 h ≈ 40 units | ~12 h ≈ 56 units |
      60 +                                                               
      61 +Plus ≈ 1 unit per session for setup. With ≈ 100 units, T4 leave
         +s room for a second run after reviewing labels.                
      62 +                                                               
      63 +## Reading the results                                         
      64 +                                                               
      65 +| Result | Meaning |                                           
      66 +|---|---|

         +or a student prototype (rough guide) |                         
      68 +| low mAP for loitering / tampering / face_covered | expected (
         +small, few scenes); rule engine + tracking compensate |        
      69 +| person / vehicle much higher in train than test | possible au
         +to-label noise → review `data/review/auto_label_check/` |      
      70 +                                                               
      71  ## Common problems

      74  |---|---|
      56 -| `No GPU` / `CUDA available: False` | Runtime → Change runtime
         - type → T4 GPU → Save; run from the top |                      
      57 -| `Not found: .../dataset.zip` | File must be exactly `MyDrive/
         -component4/dataset.zip` |                                      
      58 -| `CUDA out of memory` | Step 1: `BATCH = 16`; run Step 1 and S
         -tep 8 again |

         - up; try again later (often next day) |

⏺ Everything is ready for a high-accuracy Colab run. The full guide is in
  docs/colab_training_guide.md.

  Already in place:
  - Dataset: data/export/dataset.zip, version 2. 43,441 images, 9 classes,
    missing boxes added, every check passed.
  - Notebook: Component4_YOLO11_Training.ipynb
    (scripts/colab/Component4_YOLO11_Training.ipynb). Its settings are the most
    accurate that fit your ~100-unit budget:
    - YOLO11m at 640 px;
    - up to 100 epochs, with early stopping after 25 epochs without improvement;
    - automatic batch size and mixed precision;
    - checkpoints saved to Drive after every epoch, so it can resume.

  Steps

  1. Drive: create the folder component4_v2 and upload dataset.zip into it.
  2. Buy the plan, then check that the compute units show in Colab's Resources
     panel.
  3. Colab: File → Upload notebook → then File → Save a copy in Drive.
  4. Runtime → Change runtime type → T4 GPU (recommended, fewest units) or L4
     (faster) → Save.
  5. Runtime → Run all, and allow Drive. Check that:
     - Step 2 prints CUDA available: True;
     - Step 3 shows 30,359 / 6,502 / 6,580 images;
     - Step 5 says auto-labelling is skipped;
     - Step 8 shows Epoch 1/100.
  6. After epoch 1, run Step 8b with the units-per-hour from the Resources
     panel. It shows the real remaining hours and units.
  7. Keep the laptop awake and the tab open. If the session stops, set the same
     GPU and choose Run all; training resumes from the last epoch.
  8. When training is done, Step 9 prints per-class test scores, and Step 10
     saves best.pt and best.onnx in
     MyDrive/component4_v2/runs/c4_v2_yolo11m/weights/. The charts in that
     folder (results.png, confusion_matrix.png, PR_curve.png) are good material
     for the panel.

  Budget (estimate): on a T4, about 30–42 units (roughly 16–23 hours). On an L4,
  about 40–56 units (8–12 hours). Either fits the ~100 units, and a T4 leaves
  room for a second run after reviewing the labels.

  What to expect:
  - Main threat classes: person, vehicle, weapon, fire and smoke should score
    well.
  - Behaviour classes: loitering, tampering and face_covered will be lower,
    because they have little data.
  - Why that's acceptable: the rule engine's tracking makes up for the weak
    behaviour classes, as the threat catalogue explains to the panel.

❯ If a power cut happens, the internet connection will be gone, and the
  modelling process will stop during the process. We can't manage power cuts,
  but I think we can manage resuming properly, saving quickly, and handling
  other things, so manage the coding side for this power-cut case.

⏺ The main risk isn't training itself, it's how progress is saved. Right now the
  notebook writes checkpoints straight into Google Drive. If the Colab machine
  dies in the middle of a write, last.pt on Drive can end up half-written and
  unusable. First I'll check exactly when Ultralytics saves its checkpoints, so
  I can hook in at the right moment.

  copy it to Drive—then check how a resumed run picks its save path so I can
  restore the run folder locally.

  persisting across resumes. Now rewriting Step 8.

⏺ The notebook now handles power cuts and disconnects safely, and I tested it on
  your Mac by simulating a cut. A power cut can cost you at most the one epoch
  that was in progress (about 7–14 minutes, roughly 0.3–0.5 units). It can't
  lose or damage the run.

  What changed in Step 8 of the notebook
  (scripts/colab/Component4_YOLO11_Training.ipynb)

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Protection   │                      How it works                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fast, safe    │ Training writes to Colab's fast local disk. After every │
  │ saving        │  epoch the checkpoint, results and charts are copied to │
  │               │  Drive.                                                 │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ No            │ Each file goes to Drive as a temporary file and is      │
  │ half-written  │ renamed only when complete. A cut mid-copy leaves the   │
  │ files         │ previous good file in place.                            │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Backup        │ The previous checkpoint is kept as last_prev.pt. If the │
  │ checkpoint    │  newest one is damaged, it falls back automatically to  │
  │               │ one epoch older.                                        │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Automatic     │ On a new Colab machine, Step 8 copies the run back from │
  │ restore       │  Drive, checks the checkpoint opens, and resumes from   │
  │               │ the last saved epoch.                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ A heartbeat.json records each save. If the run saved    │
  │ No double     │ less than 25 minutes ago, Step 8 stops with a warning,  │
  │ training      │ because the old machine may still be running. Two       │
  │               │ sessions then can't overwrite each other's progress.    │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Accurate      │ Step 8b ignores the duplicate entry an epoch leaves     │
  │ progress      │ behind when it's redone after a cut.                    │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Tested:
  - cut after epoch 2: both epochs were safely in Drive;
  - immediate re-run: blocked by the guard;
  - local disk lost and the newest checkpoint damaged: it restored from Drive,
    used last_prev.pt, and finished all epochs.

  What to do after a power cut

  1. Wait until power and internet are back, then open the notebook. Don't click
     anything yet. The Colab machine runs in Google's data centre, so it may
     have kept training without your laptop.
  2. If the Step 8 cell still shows a spinning ■ and the log keeps growing: do
     nothing; it's still training.
  3. If you see "Reconnect", or no RAM/Disk bars: choose Runtime → Change
     runtime type → same GPU → Run all. It prints Restoring the run from Drive …
     → Resuming from … and carries on.
  4. If it prints STOP: … may still be running: open Runtime → Manage sessions.
     - If another session is running, leave it.
     - If none is, set FORCE_RESUME = True in Step 1, run Step 1 and Step 8
       again, then set it back to False.
  5. Never rename or delete MyDrive/component4_v2/runs/, and never change
     RUN_NAME.

  These instructions are also in docs/colab_training_guide.md, section E. I also
  corrected an earlier mistake there: changing BATCH = 16 after an
  out-of-memory error does work when resuming.

  Before you start: upload the notebook again, because the version you uploaded
  earlier doesn't have this protection. Then use File → Save a copy in Drive, so
  every later session opens this version.

❯ I want get this whole chat and attached to other project how I do that

⏺ You can do it a few ways:

  1. Export the chat to a file (simplest)
  - Type /export in Claude Code. It saves the conversation as a text file, or
    copies it to the clipboard. If the VS Code panel doesn't offer it, run
    claude in the terminal from this project folder, use /resume to pick this
    chat, then /export.
  - Copy that file into your other project's folder, e.g.
    docs/previous_chat.txt.
  - In the other project, ask Claude: "Read @docs/previous_chat.txt and continue
    from it."

  2. Use the saved transcript directly
  Claude Code keeps every chat as a .jsonl file in:
  ~/.claude/projects/-Users-vihagaviboshana-Desktop-Component4-Starter-Kit-compo
  nent4-vehicle-security/
  You can copy the newest .jsonl file into the other project. It's raw JSON,
  though, which is harder to read than an export.

  3. A handoff summary (recommended)
  This chat is very long. If you attach the whole thing, the other project's
  session will spend much of its memory just reading it. A short summary works
  better. It would cover:
  - the 9 classes and the class map;
  - the datasets used and removed;
  - every decision (from docs/decision_log.md);
  - the threat catalogue for the panel;
  - the dataset build steps;
  - the Colab training setup, including power-cut resume;
  - what's still open (the stealing re-box, the dark fire images, reviewing the
    auto labels).

  Note: /resume and claude --continue only find this chat when you start Claude
  Code inside this project folder. They don't carry it over to another project
  automatically.

  Want me to write that handoff summary as docs/project_handoff.md, so you can
  copy it into the other project?
